### `render`: values that several sections use, and guards on the rules file (lines 370 to 389)

| Line | Source | Explanation |
|---:|---|---|
| 370 | `    # =============================================================================== numbers used in several places` | Comment: a divider. Numbers that are used in several places follow. |
| 371 | `    go_start, go_end = go['states'][0]['utc'], go['states'][-1]['utc']` | `go_start`, `go_end`: the times of the first and of the last state line in the log of `go.sh`. |
| 372 | `    one_len = lambda ds: (need(len({r['min_ms'] for r in sel(recs, dataset=ds)}) == 1, 'one interval length in ' + ds),` | `one_len(ds)`: a helper, written as a lambda. A guard checks that all records of the data set `ds` have one value of `min_ms`, the minimum length of a timed interval in ms. The braces build a set that holds each value once. So its size must be 1. |
| 373 | `                          '%g s' % (sel(recs, dataset=ds)[0]['min_ms'] / 1000))[1]` | Continues line 372: the helper returns that length in seconds as text. It divides `min_ms` of the first record by 1000. `%g` prints the number without zeros at the end, for example `0.5 s` or `6 s`. |
| 374 | `    secA, secX = one_len('rerun-A'), one_len('rerun-L')      # interval lengths: part A, bwx12 parts` | `secA`: the minimum interval length of part A as text, `0.5 s`. For part A, `build_facts.py` sets `min_ms` to the constant 500 [Ref1]. `secX`: the one of part L, `6 s`. The records of the 7200 MT/s bwx12 data sets hold 6000 ms [Ref6]. By the comment these are the interval lengths of part A and of the bwx12 parts. |
| 375 | `    need(one_len('rerun-C') == secX == one_len('control-I'), 'parts L, C, I use the same interval length')` | Guard: part C and control I must have the same interval length as part L. |
| 376 | `    need(len(D['P_kB']) == 1, 'one buffer size in the P rows')` | Guard: the P rows of the 9 bwx12 files must hold exactly one buffer size. `D['P_kB']` holds the distinct values of the column `kB_total` of all P rows [Ref1]. |
| 377 | `    mib = '%g MiB' % (D['P_kB'][0] / 1024)` | `mib`: the buffer size of one thread as text. It is the value of `kB_total`, in kB, divided by 1024. The sheet prints it as `256 MiB` [Ref4]. |
| 378 | `    go2_start, go2_end = go2['states'][0]['utc'], go2['states'][-1]['utc']` | `go2_start`, `go2_end`: the times of the first and of the last state line in the log of `go2.sh`. |
| 379 | `    f72t = D['rx']['F72d12']['launches']['a6456']['utc']` | `f72t`: the launch time of the F72d12 process. It comes from the launch line with the tag `a6456` in the file `launches.txt` of that run. |
| 380 | `    rules_time = fd.dt.datetime.strptime(re.search(r'# Written (\S+)Z', fd._open(rules).read())[1], '%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S')` | `rules_time`: the time that the rules file states about itself. `fd._open(rules).read()` reads the whole file, and `_open` also notes its path for the file list. The pattern `# Written (\S+)Z` finds the text `# Written`, a space, a run of characters that are not white space (group 1) and the letter `Z`. `strptime` reads group 1 as a date and a time of the form `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SS`. |
| 381 | `    rules_lead = (go_start - rules_time).total_seconds()` | `rules_lead`: the start of `go.sh` minus `rules_time`, in seconds. |
| 382 | `    need(rules_lead > 0, 'rules written before go.sh started')` | Guard: `rules_lead` must be above 0. So the time that the rules file states is earlier than the first state line of `go.sh`. |
| 383 | `    # file times on the clock of the agent's server (the start of go.sh above is on the clock of CRB)` | Comment: file times are on the clock of the agent server. The start of `go.sh` above is on the clock of CRB. |
| 384 | `    mtime = lambda p: fd.dt.datetime.utcfromtimestamp(int(os.path.getmtime(p)))` | `mtime(p)`: a helper, written as a lambda. It returns the time of the last change of the file `p`, in UTC, in whole seconds. |
| 385 | `    rules_mtime, sha_mtime = mtime(rules), mtime(os.path.join(S, 'local.sha'))` | `rules_mtime`, `sha_mtime`: the file times of the rules file and of the file `local.sha` in `S`. |
| 386 | `    need(rules_mtime == rules_time, 'file time of the rules file equal to the time in its line 2')` | Guard: the file time of the rules file must equal `rules_time`, the time that its second line states. |
| 387 | `    need('BEFORE the run (go.sh not yet started)' in fd._open(rules).read().splitlines()[1], 'line 2 of the rules file')` | Guard: the second line of the rules file must hold the words `BEFORE the run (go.sh not yet started)`. `splitlines()[1]` is the second line of the text. |
| 388 | `    need(sha_mtime > rules_mtime, 'local.sha is newer than the rules file')` | Guard: the file `local.sha` must be newer than the rules file. |

### `render`: the drops of all pairs and the five groups of part C (lines 390 to 412)

| Line | Source | Explanation |
|---:|---|---|
| 390 | `    dl = [p['drop_asmread'] for p in N['pl']]` | `dl`: the drops of the pairs of part L in percent, asmread against oldread, one value per pair. The list holds 6 values [Ref6]. |
| 391 | `    di = [p['drop_asmread'] for p in N['pi']]` | `di`: the same for the pairs of control I, 4 values [Ref6]. |
| 392 | `    df = [p['drop_asmread'] for p in N['pf']]` | `df`: the same for the pairs of F72d12, 2 values [Ref6]. |
| 393 | `    dcr = [p['drop_read'] for p in N['pc']]` | `dcr`: the drops of `read` against oldread in the pairs of part C, 8 values [Ref6]. |
| 394 | `    dca = [p['drop_asmread'] for p in N['pc']]` | `dca`: the drops of asmread against oldread in the same pairs, 8 values [Ref6]. |
| 395 | `    dpa = [p['drop'] for p in N['pa']]` | `dpa`: the drops of the 6 pairs of part A. Each one compares the median of the 3 `bw_new read` intervals with the median of the 3 `bw_old read` intervals of the same session and round. |
| 396 | `    need(min(dl + di + df + dcr + dca + dpa) > 0, 'new kernel below old kernel in every pair')` | Guard: the lowest of all drops of the six lists must be above 0. So the new side is below the old read kernel in every pair. `+` joins the six lists to one list, which holds 34 values in this run [Ref6]. The drops of the two L-1 pairs (`N['p80']`) enter no list. |
| 397 | `    need(all(p['ratio_hi'] < 1 for p in N['pa']), 'every bw_new read interval below every bw_old read interval of its pair')` | Guard: `ratio_hi` must be below 1 in every pair of part A. So the highest `bw_new read` interval is below the lowest `bw_old read` interval of the same pair. |
| 399 | `    # part C grouped by the kernel that ran directly before (rule V2): name -> (records, drops against oldread)` | Comment: part C is grouped by the kernel that ran directly before, as rule V2 asks. Each group name maps to two lists: the records, and their drops against oldread. |
| 400 | `    Crec = sel(recs, dataset='rerun-C')` | `Crec`: all records of part C (data set `rerun-C`). |
| 402 | `    def cgroup(kernel, before):` | Starts the helper function `cgroup`, defined inside `render`. It builds one group of part C: the records of one kernel with one kernel directly before it. |
| 403 | `        rs = sel(Crec, kernel=kernel, kernel_before=before)` | `rs`: the part C records of this kernel whose field `kernel_before` equals `before`. `facts_data.py` sets `kernel_before` from the kernel sequence in the header line of the csv, not from time stamps [Ref2]. |
| 404 | `        ds = [p['drop_' + kernel] for r in rs for p in N['pc'] if p[kernel] is r]` | `ds`: for each record of `rs`, the drop of the pair in `N['pc']` that holds this same record. `is` tests for the same object. So each drop still compares the interval with the oldread interval of its own process and round. |
| 405 | `        need(len(ds) == len(rs) > 0, 'one drop per interval of a part C group')` | Guard: there must be as many drops as records, and at least one record. |
| 406 | `        return rs, ds` | Returns the two lists `rs` and `ds`. |
| 407 | `    CG = {'read after oldread': cgroup('read', 'oldread'), 'read after asmread': cgroup('read', 'asmread'),` | `CG`: the dictionary of the five groups. `read after oldread`: 4 intervals, from the cfwd processes T72C1 and T72C3 [Ref6]. `read after asmread`: 4 intervals, from the crev processes T72C2 and T72C4 [Ref6]. |
| 408 | `          'asmread after read': cgroup('asmread', 'read'), 'asmread first': cgroup('asmread', FIRST),` | Continues `CG`. `asmread after read`: 4 intervals of the cfwd processes [Ref6]. `asmread first`: 2 intervals [Ref6]. There asmread is the first interval of a crev process (round 0). The selection uses the constant `FIRST` (line 28). |
| 409 | `          'asmread after oldread': cgroup('asmread', 'oldread')}` | Ends `CG`. `asmread after oldread`: 2 intervals [Ref6]. In round 1 of a crev process asmread follows oldread, the last kernel of round 0. |
| 410 | `    N['CG'] = CG` | Stores the groups as `N['CG']`. `facts_text4.py` takes the groups from `ctx` (line 1462), not from this entry. |
| 411 | `    need(sum(len(v[0]) for v in CG.values()) == len(sel(Crec, kernel={'read', 'asmread'})), 'the five groups hold every load-only interval of part C')` | Guard: the five groups together must hold as many records as part C has `read` and asmread records. So the groups hold every load-only interval of part C. |

### `render`: record lists and range comparisons for Q2 (lines 413 to 440)

| Line | Source | Explanation |
|---:|---|---|
| 413 | `    pool = N['pool']` | `pool`: a short name for the selector function `N['pool']` (line 324). |
| 414 | `    L72o, L72a = sel(recs, dataset='rerun-L', kernel='oldread'), sel(recs, dataset='rerun-L', kernel='asmread')` | `L72o`, `L72a`: the oldread records and the asmread records of part L, 6 intervals each [Ref6]. |
| 415 | `    I72o, I72a = sel(recs, dataset='control-I', kernel='oldread'), sel(recs, dataset='control-I', kernel='asmread')` | `I72o`, `I72a`: the same two lists for control I, 4 intervals each [Ref6]. |
| 416 | `    F72o, F72a = sel(recs, dataset='F72d12', kernel='oldread'), sel(recs, dataset='F72d12', kernel='asmread')` | `F72o`, `F72a`: the same two lists for F72d12, 2 intervals each [Ref6]. |
| 417 | `    P12o, P12a = pool(BW12, 'oldread', 0), pool(BW12, 'asmread', 1)` | `P12o`: the 8000 MT/s pool of oldread at position 0 in the data sets `L-1` to `L-4` (11 intervals of 7 processes). `P12a`: asmread at position 1 in the same data sets (11 intervals). Each pool holds intervals of two lengths: 6 s in `L-1` (2 of the 11, the length of part L) and 4 s in `L-2` to `L-4` (9 of the 11) [Ref6]. |
| 418 | `    PALLo, PALLa = pool(BW12 + BW11, 'oldread', 0), pool(BW12 + BW11, 'asmread', 1)` | `PALLo`, `PALLa`: the same pools plus the bwx11 data sets `R0` and `R0b` (18 intervals each) [Ref6]. This file does not use them itself. Lines 1461 to 1462 pass them to `facts_text4.py`. |
| 419 | `    A72o = sel(recs, dataset='rerun-A', binary='bw_old', kernel='read')` | `A72o`: the records of `bw_old read` of part A, all sessions and rounds (18 intervals of 6 processes) [Ref6]. |
| 420 | `    A80o = sel(recs, dataset={'A-1', 'A-2', 'A-3'}, binary='bw_old', kernel='read')` | `A80o`: the records of `bw_old read` of the 8000 MT/s data sets `A-1`, `A-2` and `A-3` (21 intervals of 7 processes) [Ref6]. |
| 421 | `    C72o = sel(recs, dataset='rerun-C', kernel='oldread')` | `C72o`: the oldread records of part C at both of its positions, 0 in cfwd and 2 in crev (8 intervals of 4 processes) [Ref6]. |
| 422 | `    cLo, cLa = compare(L72o, P12o), compare(L72a, P12a)` | Two calls of `compare` (line 333). `cLo`: oldread of part L against `P12o`. `cLa`: asmread of part L against `P12a`. Each call takes all intervals of a list as one range from the lowest to the highest rate. Example `cLo`: 461.61 to 464.29 GB/s against 465.53 to 470.05 GB/s. The ranges do not overlap, `dist` is -1.24 GB/s, and the wording is `DIFFERS` [Ref4] [Ref6]. |
| 423 | `    cIo, cIa = compare(I72o, P12o), compare(I72a, P12a)` | `cIo`, `cIa`: oldread and asmread of control I against the same two pools. |
| 424 | `    cFo, cFa = compare(F72o, P12o), compare(F72a, P12a)` | `cFo`, `cFa`: oldread and asmread of F72d12 against the same two pools. |
| 425 | `    cAo, cCo = compare(A72o, A80o), compare(C72o, P12o)` | `cAo`: `bw_old read` of part A against `A80o`. `cCo`: oldread of part C against `P12o`. The part C list holds oldread at position 0 and at position 2. The pool holds oldread at position 0 only. None of the 8 calls of lines 422 to 425 uses a record of the new read kernel. |
| 427 | `    # part A, load-only kernels, session 1 against the same round at 8000 MT/s (rule V3)` | Comment: part A, the load-only kernels. Session 1 is compared with the same round at 8000 MT/s, as rule V3 asks. |
| 428 | `    pa3 = []` | `pa3`: the list of the results. It is empty at the start and gets 6 entries: 3 kernels x 2 rounds. `facts_text4.py` prints the 6 results in section 4.3 of the sheet and appends them to `V['q2_rows']` [Ref3]. |
| 429 | `    for b, k in (('bw_new', 'read'), ('bw_new', 'asmread'), ('bw_old', 'asmread')):` | Loop over three kernels: `bw_new read`, `bw_new asmread` and `bw_old asmread`. |
| 430 | `        for rd in (1, 2):` | Loop over the rounds 1 and 2. |
| 431 | `            s1 = sorted(sel(recs, dataset='rerun-A', session=1, round=rd, binary=b, kernel=k), key=lambda r: r['interval'])` | `s1`: the 3 intervals of session 1 of the rerun for this binary, kernel and round, sorted by the interval number. |
| 432 | `            a1 = sorted(sel(recs, dataset='A-1', round=rd, binary=b, kernel=k), key=lambda r: r['interval'])` | `a1`: the intervals of the 8000 MT/s data set `A-1` for the same binary, kernel and round. |
| 433 | `            a2 = sorted(sel(recs, dataset='A-2', round=rd, binary=b, kernel=k), key=lambda r: r['interval'])` | `a2`: the same for the data set `A-2`. `A-2` holds no record of `bw_old asmread` [Ref6]. So for that kernel the list is empty. |
| 434 | `            b80 = rng(a1 + a2)` | `b80`: the range of both lists together. For `bw_old asmread` it comes from the 3 intervals of `A-1` alone [Ref6]. |
| 435 | `            above = [r['GBps'] - b80['hi'] for r in s1 if r['GBps'] > b80['hi']]` | `above`: for each interval of `s1` above the upper end of `b80`, its distance to that end in GB/s. |
| 436 | `            below = [b80['lo'] - r['GBps'] for r in s1 if r['GBps'] < b80['lo']]` | `below`: for each interval of `s1` below the lower end of `b80`, its distance to that end in GB/s. |
| 437 | `            pa3.append(dict(binary=b, kernel=k, round=rd, s1=s1, a1=a1, a2=a2, b80=b80, above=above, below=below,` | Adds one entry to `pa3`: the binary, the kernel, the round, the three record lists, the range and the two lists of distances. |
| 438 | `                            inside=3 - len(above) - len(below)))` | Ends the entry. `inside` = 3 minus the number above minus the number below. It is the count of the intervals of `s1` that lie inside the range. |
| 439 | `    newread = [x for x in pa3 if (x['binary'], x['kernel']) == ('bw_new', 'read')]` | `newread`: the 2 entries of `bw_new read`, one per round. |

### `render`: helper `short_version`, the sentences and detail lines of section 0 (lines 477 to 564)

| Line | Source | Explanation |
|---:|---|---|
| 477 | `    def short_version(c):` | Starts the helper function `short_version`, defined inside `render`. It can read the variables of `render`. It does not run at this place. `render` calls it in line 1467 with the dictionary `ctx` as `c`. By then `section4` of `facts_text4.py` has added its results to `ctx`. The function computes no rate. It takes lowest and highest values, differences and counts from record lists that already hold `GBps`, and it rounds them for the text. |
| 478 | `        by, pcv, kF1, kI1 = c['byst'], c['partC_view'], c['kF1'], c['kI1']` | Takes four results of `section4` out of `c` [Ref3]. `by` (`c['byst']`): the oldread and asmread ranges of the 8000 MT/s pool L-1 to L-4, split by the start state of the process. `pcv` (`c['partC_view']`): oldread of part C compared with the pool processes that started after 120-thread work. `kF1`, `kI1`: asmread of F72d12 and of control I, each compared with the data set L-1 alone. |
| 479 | `        kAao, oth = c['kAao'], c['byst_other']` | Takes two more results [Ref3]. `kAao`: the part C asmread intervals that ran directly after oldread, compared with the asmread pool of L-1 to L-4. `oth` (`c['byst_other']`): oldread ranges of the runners `bwx11` and `bwx` for processes that started after 120-thread work. |
| 480 | `        need(not kAao['overlap'] and kAao['dist'] > 0, 'part C: asmread after oldread above the 8000 MT/s pool')` | Guard: the comparison `kAao` must show no overlap and a distance above 0. Then every part C asmread interval that ran directly after oldread is above the highest asmread rate of the 8000 MT/s pool. In a comparison, the key `a` is the 7200 MT/s range, `b` is the 8000 MT/s range, and `dist` is the distance of the nearest ends in GB/s (line 336). |
| 481 | `        a_lo = sum(1 for r in A72o if r['GBps'] < cAo['b']['lo'])` | `a_lo`: the number of intervals of `A72o` whose `GBps` is below the lowest 8000 MT/s rate. `A72o` holds the `bw_old read` intervals of part A from all sessions and rounds (line 419). `cAo['b']` is the range of `bw_old read` in the 8000 MT/s data sets A-1 to A-3 (line 425). The test is strict, and it uses the values before rounding. |
| 482 | `        a_hi = sum(1 for r in A72o if r['GBps'] > cAo['b']['hi'])` | `a_hi`: the number of intervals of `A72o` whose `GBps` is above the highest 8000 MT/s rate. This test is strict too. |
| 483 | `        need(a_lo > 0 and a_hi > 0 and cAo['a']['hi'] - cAo['a']['lo'] > cAo['b']['hi'] - cAo['b']['lo'],` | Guard: at least one interval must lie on each side. Also the 7200 MT/s range (highest minus lowest rate, in GB/s) must be wider than the 8000 MT/s range. |
| 484 | `             'part A, old read: the 7200 MT/s range is wider than the 8000 MT/s range on both sides')` | The message of the guard of line 483. |
| 485 | `        need(inside(cFa), 'asmread: F72d12 inside the range of the 8000 MT/s pool')` | Guard: the asmread range of F72d12 must lie inside the asmread range of the 8000 MT/s pool (`inside`, line 468). |
| 486 | `        nL, nP = nns(L72o), nn(cLo['b'])` | `nL`: the text for n of the oldread intervals of part L. `nns` writes it as intervals / processes / sessions / boots. `nP`: the text for n of the 8000 MT/s oldread pool. `nn` writes it as intervals / processes / boots. |
| 487 | `        need(nns(L72a) == nL and nn(cLa['b']) == nP, 'part L: the same n for oldread and asmread on both sides')` | Guard: the asmread side must give the same two texts. So the sentences below use `nL` and `nP` for both kernels. |
| 488 | `        short = [` | `short`: the list of the three sentences of the Short version. It starts here. |
| 489 | `            'Q1: yes, at 7200 MT/s with 12 memory modules the new read kernel was still below the old read kernel: %s '` | Sentence 1 answers Q1 from the `read` intervals of part C. It says that at 7200 MT/s with 12 memory modules the new read kernel was still below the old read kernel. The word `yes` is fixed text. The guard of line 396 checks it: every drop must be above 0. The `%s` gets the drops of the group `read after oldread`. |
| 490 | `            'lower when it ran directly after the old read kernel and %s lower when it ran directly after asmread '` | Continues sentence 1: the first drops are those of `read` directly after the old read kernel. The `%s` of this line gets the drops of the group `read after asmread`. In that group (the crev processes) the oldread interval of the pair is the last of its round. It ran after the `read` interval. |
| 491 | `            '(part C, %s runs, %d pairs each).' % (pcr(c_ro[1]), pcr(c_ra[1]), secX, len(c_ro[1])),` | Ends sentence 1: it names part C, the interval length and the number of pairs per group. Then the values. `pcr(c_ro[1])` and `pcr(c_ra[1])` print the lowest and the highest drop of each group with 1 decimal, half up. One drop is `(1 - GBps of read / GBps of oldread) x 100`, in percent, and both intervals come from the same process and round (lines 309 to 314). `secX` is the interval length of the bwx12 parts as text. `len(c_ro[1])` is the number of pairs per group. The guard of line 471 checks that both groups hold the same number. |
| 492 | `            'Q2, old read kernel: by the rule fixed before the run the result against the 8000 MT/s runs is "%s" in '` | Sentence 2 is about the old read kernel in Q2. It gives the result against the 8000 MT/s runs by the rule that was fixed before the run. The `%s` gets the wording `SAME`. The guard of line 464 checks that part A (`cAo`) and part C (`cCo`) overlap their 8000 MT/s ranges. |
| 493 | `            'the repeat of the 2026-11-13 test (part A, where the test cannot exclude a difference of about %s %%, '` | Continues sentence 2: the wording holds in part A, which the text calls the repeat of the test of 2026-11-13. The `%s` gets the size of a difference that the test cannot exclude there, in percent. |
| 494 | `            'est.) and in part C (about %s %%, est.), and the rate was lower in part L (%s GB/s, n = %s, against '` | Continues sentence 2: the same size for part C. Both sizes are marked est. Then the words that the rate was lower in part L, with the range and n of part L. These words are fixed text. The guard of line 465 checks them. |
| 495 | `            '%s GB/s, n = %s), but the data do not show that the boot or the memory speed is the cause.'` | Ends the text of sentence 2: the range and n of the 8000 MT/s pool. The last clause says that the data do not show the boot or the memory speed as the cause. |
| 496 | `            % (SAME, pc(cAo['width']), pc(cCo['width']), sp(cLo['a']), nL, sp(cLo['b']), nP),` | The values for sentence 2. `pc(cAo['width'])`, `pc(cCo['width'])`: the width of each 8000 MT/s range, `(hi - lo) / lo x 100`, in percent with 1 decimal. `sp(cLo['a'])`: the lowest and the highest oldread rate of part L. `sp(cLo['b'])`: the lowest and the highest rate of the 8000 MT/s oldread pool (L-1 to L-4, position 0). `sp` prints each end with 2 decimals. `nL` and `nP` are the two texts for n (line 486). |
| 497 | `            'Q2, new read kernel: asmread (the hand-written loop with the same loads, used in place of the new read '` | Sentence 3 is about the load-only loop in Q2. It uses asmread in place of the new read kernel and says so in brackets. |
| 498 | `            'kernel) ran above every bwx12 row of the fixed 8000 MT/s list in part L (%s GB/s, n = %s, against %s GB/s, '` | Continues sentence 3: asmread ran above every bwx12 row of the fixed 8000 MT/s list. These words are fixed text. Guards check them: line 462 for part L, line 480 for part C, and a guard in `section4` for control I [Ref3]. The placeholders get the asmread range and n of part L, and the asmread range of the pool. |
| 499 | `            'n = %s), in control I (%s GB/s, n = %s, %s) and in the %d part C intervals that ran directly after the old '` | Continues sentence 3: the n of the pool. Then control I with its asmread range, its n and the label `ADDED`. Then the number of the part C intervals that ran directly after the old read kernel. |

Its row starts with the name of the state line: the log name, the words `log, line`, the line number in the log and the label of the line. Its pass limit is a difference below 0.05 GB/s. It tests only the 9 bwx12 files of the rerun [Ref1]. ### `render`: section 1.5, point 12, Linux settings of the two boots (lines 1030 to 1058)

| Line | Source | Explanation |
|---:|---|---|
| 1030 | `    # settings that were compared between the two boots and found equal` | Comment: the next lines handle the settings that were compared between the two boots and found equal. |
| 1031 | `    pre = p13['pre']` | `pre`: the record of the save of Linux settings in the boot of 2026-11-09 (`p13['pre']`). It holds the time of the save and the texts of its files. |
| 1032 | `    cmd72 = go['other']['cmdline'][0]` | `cmd72`: the Linux kernel command line of the rerun, from the log of `go.sh`. |
| 1033 | `    rel72 = re.search(r'vmlinuz-(\S+)', cmd72)[1]` | `rel72`: the release text of the Linux kernel in that command line. The pattern takes the run of characters that are not white space after `vmlinuz-` (group 1). |
| 1034 | `    thp80 = re.search(r'^## thp=(.*) defrag=(.*)$', D['env_partA'], re.M)` | `thp80`: the huge page setting in the file `env.txt` of the A-2 run (`D['env_partA']`). The pattern matches a whole line of the form `## thp=... defrag=...`. Group 1 is the value of `thp`, and group 2 is the value of `defrag`. `re.M` makes `^` and `$` match at the start and at the end of every line. |
| 1035 | `    need(inboot(pre['when']) == inboot(a1first), 'the settings were saved in the boot of the 8000 MT/s baselines')` | Guard: the settings must have been saved in the boot of the 8000 MT/s baselines. The time of the save and `a1first` must give the same boot number (line 1000). |
| 1036 | `    need(p13['journal_release'] == rel72, 'same Linux kernel release in both boots')` | Guard: the Linux kernel release in the saved journal of the boot of 2026-11-09 must equal `rel72`. |
| 1037 | `    need(pre['cmdline'] == cmd72, 'same Linux kernel command line in both boots')` | Guard: the saved command line must equal `cmd72`. |

### `render`: section 1.5, firmware records and their guards (lines 1059 to 1085)

| Line | Source | Explanation |
|---:|---|---|
| 1059 | `    # firmware settings: what the saved replies and lists of the settings tool show` | Comment: the firmware settings follow. The lines show what the saved replies and lists of the settings tool hold. |
| 1060 | `    fw = D['fw']` | `fw`: the records of the firmware settings tool (`D['fw']`). |
| 1061 | `    fb, fx, fr = fw['lists']['base'], fw['lists']['x1'], fw['lists']['revert']` | `fb`, `fx`, `fr`: the three saved settings lists: from the baseline boot, after the first change of a setting, and after the set-back. The set-back is the step that set the two changed settings to their earlier values again. |
| 1062 | `    sx1, sx2, srv, sdm, fin = fw['sets']['x1'], fw['sets']['x2'], fw['sets']['revert'], fw['sets']['dimm'], fw['final']` | `sx1`, `sx2`, `srv`, `sdm`: the four reply files `set.txt`. A reply file holds what the settings tool answered to a change. `fin`: the last reading of the memory speed. |
| 1063 | `    one_set = lambda v: (need(len(v['set']) == 1 and v['set'][0]['modified'] is True, 'one set reply, modified: true'), v['set'][0])[1]` | `one_set(v)`: a helper, written as a lambda. Its guard checks that the file holds exactly one reply to a change and that this reply says `modified: true`. Then it returns that reply. The form with the tuple is the one of line 286. |
| 1064 | `    x1s, x2s, dms = one_set(sx1), one_set(sx2), one_set(sdm)` | `x1s`, `x2s`, `dms`: the one reply of the file of the folder `x1`, of the folder `x2`, and of the memory speed change. |
| 1065 | `    rvs = {x['name']: x for x in srv['set']}` | `rvs`: the replies to changes in the file of the set-back, as a dictionary with the name of the setting as the key. |
| 1066 | `    rvg = {x['name']: x for x in srv['get']}` | `rvg`: the replies to readings in the same file, by the name of the setting. |
| 1067 | `    need(fb['path'] == p13['q_path'] and fb['n'] == fr['n'] == fx['n'] and fw['same_entries'] and not fw['same_order']` | Starts a guard on the lists. The baseline list must be the file that point 10 reads (`q_path`). The three lists must hold the same number of entries. The baseline list and the list after the set-back must hold the same entries, in another order. |
| 1068 | `         and fb['unavailable'] == fr['unavailable'] and fb['val'] == fr['val'],` | Continues the guard: these two lists must hold the same number of entries without a value. They must also hold the same values for the three settings that `build_facts.py` reads: `TXREQ Max`, `TXREQ Min` and `DIMM Frequency`. |
| 1069 | `         'the settings list after the set-back holds the same entries as the list of the baseline boot')` | The message of the guard: the settings list after the set-back holds the same entries as the list of the baseline boot. |
| 1070 | `    need((x1s['name'], x2s['name'], dms['name']) == ('TXREQ Min', 'TXREQ Max', 'DIMM Frequency')` | Starts a guard on the changes. The three single replies must name the settings `TXREQ Min` (folder `x1`), `TXREQ Max` (folder `x2`) and `DIMM Frequency`. |
| 1071 | `         and sorted(rvs) == sorted(rvg) == ['TXREQ Max', 'TXREQ Min'] and all(x['modified'] is True for x in srv['set'])` | Continues the guard: the file of the set-back must hold changes and readings of exactly the two TXREQ settings, and every change must say `modified: true`. |
| 1072 | `         and all(rvs[n]['answer'] == rvg[n]['answer'] == fb['val'][n] for n in rvs)` | Continues the guard: for both settings, the value that was set, the value that was read back and the value of the baseline list must be equal. |
| 1073 | `         and x1s['answer'] != fb['val']['TXREQ Min'] and x2s['answer'] != fb['val']['TXREQ Max'],` | Continues the guard: the values of the two changes for the other test must differ from the baseline values. |
| 1074 | `         'TXREQ Min and TXREQ Max were changed and then set back to the values of the list of the baseline boot')` | The message of the guard: `TXREQ Min` and `TXREQ Max` were changed and then set back to the values of the list of the baseline boot. |
| 1075 | `    need(sx1['when'] < sx2['when'] < srv['when'] < fr['mtime'] < sdm['mtime'] < try6['first_log_utc'] < fin['mtime']` | Starts a guard on the order in time. It must be: the first change, the second change, the set-back, the file time of the list after the set-back, the file time of the reply to the memory speed change, the first Linux kernel log line of the boot of the rerun, and the file time of the last reading. |
| 1076 | `         and fb['mtime'] < sx1['when'] < fx['mtime'] < sx2['when'] and sdm['when'] is None` | Continues the guard: the file time of the baseline list lies before the first change. The file time of the list after the first change lies between the two changes. The reply file of the memory speed change holds no date line (`when` is `None`). |
| 1077 | `         and try6['boots_first'] <= fin['mtime'] <= try6['boots_last'],` | Continues the guard: the file time of the last reading lies inside the span of row 0 of `boots.txt`. |
| 1078 | `         'order in time of the replies and lists of the settings tool')` | The message of the guard. |
| 1079 | `    need([k for k in fx['val'] if fx['val'][k] != fb['val'][k]] == ['TXREQ Min'] and fx['val']['TXREQ Min'] == x1s['answer']` | Starts a guard on the list after the first change. Of the three settings that are read, it may differ from the baseline list in `TXREQ Min` only, and its value must be the answer of the first change. |
| 1080 | `         and len(fw['x1_diff']) == 2, 'the list after the first change differs in TXREQ Min only')` | Ends the guard: the two lists must differ in exactly 2 entry texts, which are the old and the new form of one entry (`x1_diff`). The message follows. |
| 1081 | `    need(dms['answer'] == '%d MT/s' % try6['speed_MTps'] == fin['answer'] and fb['val']['DIMM Frequency'] == '%d MT/s' % p13['q_speed'],` | Guard on `DIMM Frequency`. The reply to the change and the last reading must both hold the speed of the file `try6.txt`, which is 7200 MT/s in that file. The baseline list must hold the speed that point 10 prints. In the file `questions.json` of the folder `uefisettings-20261117` that speed is 8000 MT/s. Point 10 reads its value from the same file as the baseline list. So this part of the guard compares two readings of one file. The guard holds neither of the two numbers as a fixed value. |
| 1082 | `         'DIMM Frequency: 8000 MT/s in the lists, set to 7200 MT/s, read as 7200 MT/s')` | The message of the guard. It names the two speeds, 8000 MT/s and 7200 MT/s, as fixed text. |
| 1083 | `    need('It needs root rights.' in dimm_txt and 'reads and writes firmware settings' in dimm_txt` | Starts a guard on the DIMM report. The report must hold two text pieces: that the tool needs root rights, and that it reads and writes firmware settings. |
| 1084 | ``         and 'State of CRB after the test (2026-11-17 20:06): "DIMM Frequency" = `7200 MT/s`' in dimm_txt,`` | Continues the guard: the report must also hold its sentence on the state of CRB after the test, with the setting `DIMM Frequency` at 7200 MT/s. |
| 1085 | `         'DIMM report: the settings tool needs root, and the last reading is the setting "DIMM Frequency"')` | The message of the guard. |

### `render`: section 3.2, parts L and I and the earlier run F72d12 (lines 1306 to 1352)

| Line | Source | Explanation |
|---:|---|---|
| 1306 | `    # ---- 3.2` | Comment: a divider for section 3.2. |
| 1307 | `    w('### 3.2 Parts L and I: bwx12, common stop, %s, oldread then asmread' % secX)` | Text of the sheet: the heading of section 3.2. It names parts L and I, `bwx12`, the common stop, the interval length and the order oldread then asmread. `%s` gets `secX`, the interval length of the bwx12 parts as text (`6 s` in the facts sheet [Ref4]). The words `oldread then asmread` are fixed text. No line of this file tests the position of these two kernels in part L, in control I or in F72d12. The records agree with the words: in each of these processes oldread is at position 0 and asmread is at position 1 of both rounds. In round 1, oldread runs directly after `read_1pl`, the last kernel of round 0 [Ref6]. The words `common stop` are fixed text too (line 1354). |
| 1308 | `    w('')` | Adds an empty line. |
| 1309 | `    w('- In these parts asmread is used in place of the new read kernel. It is the same loop: 8 "ldp q" loads per 256 B.')` | Bullet 1, fixed text: in these parts asmread is used in place of the new read kernel, and it is the same loop: 8 `ldp q` loads per 256 B. This script reads no machine code. The saved machine-code listing of `bwx12` agrees with the bullet. That listing is the file `listing.txt` in the build record `crb-build-1` of `bwx12` (build of 2026-11-15). It is output of the tool `objdump`, which prints the machine code of a binary as assembler text. The file `build.txt` of that build record holds the sha256 of this `listing.txt` and the sha256 of `bwx12` that `go.sh` tests. In `k_asmread` and in `k_read`, one loop pass holds 8 `ldp` instructions on `q` registers and moves the pointer by 256 bytes. |
| 1310 | `    w('- The drop is computed inside the same process and round: (1 - asmread / oldread) x 100.')` | Bullet 2, fixed text: the drop is computed inside the same process and round, as `(1 - asmread / oldread) x 100`. Line 314 computes the drop with this formula. |
| 1311 | `    w('- "m0 + m1" are the sums over the threads of mesh 0 and of mesh 1.')` | Bullet 3, fixed text: `m0 + m1` are the sums over the threads of mesh 0 and of mesh 1. |
| 1312 | `    w('')` | Adds an empty line. |
| 1313 | `    rows = []` | `rows`: a new empty list for the table of section 3.2. |

| 1316 | `        o, a_ = p['old'], p['asmread']` | `o`: the oldread record of the pair. `a_`: the asmread record of the same process and round. |
| 1317 | `        return [p['tag'] if p['dataset'] != 'F72d12' else 'F72d12', what, p['round'], '%.2f' % o['load1_at_launch'], g(o['GBps']),` | Returns the row. It starts with the tag: the folder tag, or the fixed text `F72d12` for that data set. The tag of an F72d12 record is the name of its csv file without `.csv`, which is `a6456`. Then `what`, the round, the load at launch with 2 decimals, and the oldread rate `g(o['GBps'])`. The rate is the sum of `bytes / elapsed_s / 1e9` over the T rows of one interval (one row per thread), printed with 2 decimals. |
| 1318 | `                '%s + %s' % (g(o['mesh0_GBps']), g(o['mesh1_GBps'])), g(a_['GBps']),` | Continues the row: `m0 + m1` of oldread, the sums over the threads of mesh 0 and of mesh 1. Each value is rounded separately. So the two printed values can add up to 0.01 GB/s more or less than the printed rate. Then the asmread rate `g(a_['GBps'])`. |

It is the reference number of the csv and the line numbers of the two I rows. The printed rate is not the value of the I row. The I row holds the bytes of all threads divided by the longest elapsed time of one thread. ### `render`: section 3.3, part C (lines 1353 to 1395)

| Line | Source | Explanation |
|---:|---|---|
| 1353 | `    # ---- 3.3` | Comment: a divider for section 3.3. |
| 1354 | `    w('### 3.3 Part C: bwx12, common stop, %s, oldread, read and asmread in one process' % secX)` | Text of the sheet: the heading of section 3.3. It names part C, `bwx12`, the common stop, the interval length (`secX`), and oldread, read and asmread in one process. The words `common stop` are fixed text here and in the heading of section 3.2. No line of this file reads the field `stop` (the stop rule) of the records. Every bwx12 record of the rerun holds `common` in that field [Ref6]. It is the value of `stop=` in the header line of the csv [Ref8]. |
| 1355 | `    w('')` | Adds an empty line. |
| 1356 | ``    w('- `read` is the new read kernel in bwx12. `oldread` is the old read kernel.')`` | Bullet 1, fixed text: `read` is the new read kernel in `bwx12`, and `oldread` is the old read kernel. This script reads no machine code. The saved listings agree with the bullet. The listing of `bwx12` (line 1309) holds `k_read` and `k_oldread`. The file `probe.txt` in the result folder of baseline A-1 (`kernels-20261113T181222Z`) holds `k_read` of `bw_new` and `k_read` of `bw_old`. Both files describe the binaries of the rerun: line 1309 gives the proof for the listing, and that `probe.txt` holds the sha256 values of `bw_old` and `bw_new` that `ra.sh` tests. `k_read` of `bwx12` and `k_read` of `bw_new` hold the same 13 instructions. `k_oldread` of `bwx12` and `k_read` of `bw_old` hold the same 70 instructions. The listings differ only in addresses and in the function name that a label shows. First, each function lies at another place in its binary. So the address of each line differs, and so does the printed target of each branch (1 branch in the new read kernel, 3 in the old one). The distance from the function start to the target is equal. Second, 3 instructions of the old read kernel name an address outside the function: two `adrp` instructions (each forms the address of a memory page) and one `bl` instruction (a function call). These 3 lie outside the loop. |

| 1446 | `           rng(g_ra[0])['procs'], tags(g_ra[0]), pcr(dl), len(dl), gL['procs'], gL['sessions'], secA, pcr(dpa), len(dpa),` | Continues the values: the number of processes and the folder tags of `g_ra`. For part L: `pcr(dl)`, the number of pairs, and the processes and sessions from `gL`. For part A: `secA`, `pcr(dpa)` and the number of pairs. |
| 1447 | `           gA['sessions'], len(dcr), len(dpa), len(dl), len(dca), pcr(CG['asmread after read'][1]),` | Continues the values: the number of sessions of part A from `gA`. Then the four counts for the words `in all N pairs`: `len(dcr)`, `len(dpa)`, `len(dl)` and `len(dca)`. Then `pcr` of the group `asmread after read`. |
| 1448 | `           pcr(CG['asmread first'][1]), pcr(CG['asmread after oldread'][1]), ADDED, NOT_PLAN, pcr(di), pcr(df)))` | Ends the values: `pcr` of the groups `asmread first` and `asmread after oldread`, the fixed texts `ADDED` and `NOT_PLAN` about control I, `pcr(di)` for control I and `pcr(df)` for F72d12. |
| 1449 | `    w('**Verdict.** ' + V['q1'])` | Text of the sheet: the verdict, after the bold word `Verdict.`. |
| 1450 | `    w('')` | Adds an empty line. |
| 1451 | `    so, sa = rng(L72o + I72o + F72o), rng(L72a + I72a + F72a)` | `so`: the range of the oldread records of part L, control I and F72d12 taken together. `sa`: the same for asmread. |
| 1452 | `    w('- In the table of section 3.2 the lowest and the highest oldread value are %s GB/s apart (%s and %s GB/s). The '` | A bullet after the verdict: in the table of section 3.2 the lowest and the highest oldread value are a number of GB/s apart. The two values follow in brackets. |
| 1453 | `      'lowest and the highest asmread value are %s GB/s apart (%s and %s GB/s). So the size of the drop follows the '` | Continues the bullet: the same for the lowest and the highest asmread value. A last sentence on the size of the drop starts. |
| 1454 | `      'load-only loop.' % (g(so['hi'] - so['lo']), g(so['lo']), g(so['hi']), g(sa['hi'] - sa['lo']), g(sa['lo']), g(sa['hi'])))` | Ends the text of the bullet: the size of the drop follows the load-only loop. This sentence is fixed text. No guard compares the two differences. The values: `hi - lo`, `lo` and `hi` of each range. Each difference is computed before rounding and then printed with 2 decimals. In the facts sheet the two differences are 7.59 GB/s for oldread and 26.03 GB/s for asmread [Ref4]. |
| 1455 | `    w('')` | Adds an empty line. |

### `render`: sections 4 to 6 through `section4`, then the Short version (lines 1457 to 1468)

| Line | Source | Explanation |
|---:|---|---|
| 1457 | `    # =============================================================================== 4` | Comment: a divider. Section 4 of the sheet follows. |
| 1458 | `    from facts_text4 import section4` | Imports `section4` from `facts_text4.py` here, inside `render`. `facts_text4.py` itself imports helper functions from this file in its first lines [Ref3]. With an import at the top of both files, Python raises an `ImportError`: each file would need names of the other before they are defined. |
| 1459 | `    ctx = dict(` | `ctx`: a dictionary with what `section4` needs from this file. It starts here. |
| 1460 | `        cLo=cLo, cLa=cLa, cIo=cIo, cIa=cIa, cFo=cFo, cFa=cFa, cAo=cAo, cCo=cCo, pa3=pa3, newread=newread,` | Entries of `ctx`: the 8 comparisons `cLo` to `cCo` (lines 422 to 425), the list `pa3` and its part `newread` (lines 428 to 439). |
| 1461 | `        L72o=L72o, L72a=L72a, I72o=I72o, I72a=I72a, F72o=F72o, F72a=F72a, P12o=P12o, P12a=P12a, PALLo=PALLo,` | Continues `ctx`: the record lists of part L, of control I and of F72d12, the two 8000 MT/s pools `P12o` and `P12a`, and the pool `PALLo`. |
| 1462 | `        PALLa=PALLa, A72o=A72o, A80o=A80o, C72o=C72o, rules=rules, evd=evd, dl=dl, di=di, df=df, CG=CG)` | Ends `ctx`: the pool `PALLa`, the record lists `A72o`, `A80o` and `C72o`, the path `rules`, the steps `evd`, the drop lists `dl`, `di` and `df`, and the groups `CG`. |
| 1463 | `    ctx.update(extra)` | Adds the two entries that section 1.5 stored in `extra` (line 1017 and line 1128). |
| 1464 | `    section4(D, N, V, L, ref, q2row, ctx)` | Calls `section4`. It appends sections 4, 5 and 6 to `L` and fills `V` [Ref3]. It adds rows to `V['q2_rows']` through `q2row` (line 359) and sets the verdict for Q2 [Ref3]. It also adds its own results to `ctx`, which `short_version` reads (lines 478 to 479). |
| 1465 | `    need(sum(1 for r_ in V['q2_rows'] if r_['part'] == 'C' and r_['decides']) == 2,` | Starts a guard: exactly 2 rows of `V['q2_rows']` must have the part `C` and a true `decides`. Decision (r) states this number (line 1225). |
| 1466 | `         'two rows of part C carry a wording by rule V1 point 2 (decision (r))')` | The message of the guard: two rows of part C carry a wording by rule V1 point 2. |
| 1467 | `    L[i_short:i_short] = short_version(ctx)       # section 0, written last` | Inserts the lines that `short_version(ctx)` returns at the index `i_short` (line 474). The slice `L[i_short:i_short]` is empty, so no line is replaced. So the Short version stands in section 0, and it is built after section 6. The comment says: section 0, written last. |

### `render`: section 7, the file list, the date check and the return value (lines 1469 to 1488)

| Line | Source | Explanation |
|---:|---|---|
| 1469 | `    # =============================================================================== 7` | Comment: a divider. Section 7 of the sheet follows. |
| 1470 | `    w('## 7. Files')` | Text of the sheet: the heading of section 7, `## 7. Files`. |
| 1471 | `    w('')` | Adds an empty line. |
| 1472 | `    w('Every raw file and script that this sheet uses, one line each. R and S are the folders named at the top.')` | Text of the sheet, fixed text: the list holds every raw file and script that the sheet uses, one line each. `R` and `S` are the folders named at the top of the sheet. |
| 1473 | `    w('')` | Adds an empty line. |
| 1474 | `    missing = [p for p in dict.fromkeys(fd.OPENED) if os.path.abspath(p) not in ref.d]` | `missing`: the opened paths that are not registered in `ref.d`. `fd.OPENED` holds every path that the function `_open` of `facts_data.py` opened. `build_facts.py` appended more paths to it: the `stderr.txt` files, and the `threads120.csv` files of its cross-check with `stat_k120.py` [Ref1]. `dict.fromkeys` keeps each path once. |
| 1475 | `    need(not missing, 'every file that the script opened is in the file list: %s' % missing)` | Guard: `missing` must be empty. When a path is missing, the script stops and names it. The guard does not test a file that was opened without `_open`. `stat_bwx.py` opens the csv files of its folders in that way [Ref10]. |
| 1476 | `    for key, (n, desc) in ref.d.items():` | Loop over the registered entries of `ref.d`, in the order of the reference numbers. A dictionary of Python keeps the order of insertion. |
| 1477 | `        if key.startswith('ext:'):` | Tests whether the key starts with `ext:`, which marks an external source. |
| 1478 | `            w('- [Ref%d] %s' % (n, desc))` | If so: writes a line with the reference number and the description only. |
| 1479 | `        else:` | If not, the entry is a file: |
| 1480 | ``            w('- [Ref%d] `%s`: %s' % (n, rel(key), desc))`` | Writes a line with the reference number, the path and the description. `rel` shortens a path under `R` or `S` to `R/...` or `S/...`. A file outside both folders keeps its full path. |
| 1481 | `    w('')` | Adds an empty line. |
| 1482 | ``    w('Output files of this sheet: `S/FACTS.md` (this file) and `S/facts.json` (every interval as one record, plus '`` | Text of the sheet: the last line names the two output files, `S/FACTS.md` and `S/facts.json`. For the second it says in brackets what it holds: every interval as one record. |
| 1483 | `      '"meta" and "verdicts").')` | Ends that line: `facts.json` also holds the parts `meta` and `verdicts`. Fixed text. |
| 1484 | `    w('')` | Adds an empty line. |
| 1485 | `    text = '\n'.join(L)` | `text`: all lines of `L`, joined with newline characters to one text. |
| 1486 | `    bad = check_dates(text)` | `bad`: the result of `check_dates` (line 79). It holds every sentence or table cell that holds a time of another day without a date. |
| 1487 | `    need(OTHER_DAY and not bad, 'a time of another day stands without its date in the same sentence or cell: %s' % bad[:3])` | Guard: `OTHER_DAY` must not be empty, and `bad` must be empty. So the script stops when no time of another day was noted at all, or when such a time stands without its date. The message shows the first 3 pieces of `bad`. |
| 1488 | `    return V, text` | Returns the verdict object `V` and the text. The function `render` and the file end here. `build_facts.py` then writes `V` into `facts.json` and the text into FACTS.md [Ref1]. |

## References

- [Ref1] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/tmp/agent-scratch/w120-7200-2026-11-17/build_facts.py`: The caller. `main` (lines 1033 to 1052) calls `render(D)` and then writes facts.json and FACTS.md. `collect` (lines 50 to 290) builds the records: `add_k120` (lines 119 to 140) sets `GBps` of a part A record, `add_bwx` (lines 142 to 160) sets `GBps` of a bwx record. Lines 30 and 31 hold the two filters: oldread at position 0 with asmread at position 1, and the kernels oldread, read, asmread. `run_checks` (line 653) runs the integrity checks. `write_json` (lines 899 to 1030) rounds the floating-point numbers of `V` and of the records to 3 decimals. The files `A1.csv` and `A3.csv` of the data sets A-2 and A-3 are named in lines 187 to 189. Lines 188 and 189 keep only the processes of `A3.csv` with the tags `onebuf-ms500-1` to `onebuf-ms500-3`. Lines 207 to 221 name the csv files of each earlier bwx data set that rx.sh or r0.sh wrote. The integrity check `I-agg` is in lines 736 to 740. Lines 83, 234 and 805 add paths to the list `OPENED` of facts_data.py. Line 820 calls `interval_rows` of stat_bwx.py for each folder of the integrity check `x-bwx`. Lines 258 to 267 name the csv file and the tag prefix of each data set of the first runner bwx. Line 276 keeps only the processes whose tag starts with that prefix. Lines 121 to 129 set `kernel_before` of a part A record. Lines 592 to 596 read the header lines of `one-thread.csv`. Line 614 and lines 521 to 524 read the header lines of `A2.csv`, `A5.csv` and `A6.csv` with `step_headers` of facts_data.py.
- [Ref2] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/tmp/agent-scratch/w120-7200-2026-11-17/facts_data.py`: The parsers and range functions. `read_k120` (lines 264 to 304) reads the interval rows of a part A csv and skips the row `best` (line 298). `read_bwx` (lines 313 to 353) and `bwx_intervals` (lines 356 to 383) read the T rows and add the thread rates. `sel` (line 442), `rng` (line 461), `overlap` (line 471), `gap` (line 475), `width_pct` (line 484). `_open` (line 39) opens a file and adds its path to the list `OPENED` (line 36). `read_k120` stores a row with the first field `thread` apart from the interval rows (lines 294 to 297).
- [Ref3] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/tmp/agent-scratch/w120-7200-2026-11-17/facts_text4.py`: The second text module. Lines 11 and 12 import helper functions from facts_text.py. `section4` (line 24) writes sections 4, 5 and 6. Its guard in line 1461 stops the script when an integrity check failed. `section4` sets `V['q2']` (line 1379), `V['insufficient_data']` (line 1554) and `V['hypotheses']` (line 1612).
- [Ref4] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/tmp/agent-scratch/w120-7200-2026-11-17/FACTS.md`: The output text. Section 0 starts at line 12, the word list at line 38, section 1 at line 98, section 2 at line 275, section 3 at line 303, section 4 at line 436 and section 7 at line 950.
- [Ref5] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/tmp/agent-scratch/w120-7200-2026-11-17/baselines-and-rules.txt`: The fixed list of 8000 MT/s baselines and the rules V1 to V4, F1 and F2. Point 2 of the rule of finding V1 gives the two wordings. Lines 28 to 32 list the csv files of the rows L-1 to L-4.
- [Ref6] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/tmp/agent-scratch/w120-7200-2026-11-17/facts.json`: The output that holds `V` under `verdicts`: 9 entries in `q1_parts` and 42 entries in `q2_rows`. Its part `intervals` holds the records. The data set A-2 has no record of `bw_old asmread` there. Its part `meta` counts the records of each of the 26 data sets (`counts`, `by_dataset`). The field `min_ms` of a record gives its interval length: 6000 ms in the records of L-1 and of the 7200 MT/s bwx12 data sets, 4000 ms in the records of L-2 to L-4. The field `process` of a record of the data set `rerun-A` numbers the 24 processes of part A in its session from 1. It is 1 for `bw_old read` and 2 for `bw_new read` in round 1, and 24 and 23 in round 2, in all three sessions. In the records of `bw_old read`, `process` is 1 in round 1 and 24 in round 2 for A-1, and 1 and 12 for A-2. A-1 holds 24 processes and A-2 holds 12. In the records of the second and the third process of A-3, `kernel_before` starts with `not listed`. In the records of `bw_new read` of round 2, `process` and `kernel_before` are 23 and `bw_old write` in `rerun-A` and in A-1, and 11 and `bw_new asmread` in A-2.
- [Ref7] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/results/k120-T72A1-20261117T221209Z/threads120.csv`: Example of a part A file. Line 2 gives the formula of `aggregate_GBps`. Line 3 names the columns. Line 4 is an interval row with the rate 467.83 GB/s in column 7.
- [Ref8] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/results/w120-T72L1-20261117T221329Z/a6456.csv`: Example of a bwx12 file. Line 4 names the columns of a T row: `cpu` is column 6, `bytes` is column 7, `elapsed_s` is column 8. Line 3 names the columns of an I row: `total_bytes` is column 7, `max_elapsed_s` is column 8, `aggregate_GBps` is column 9. Line 128 is the I row of the first oldread interval. Its `total_bytes` divided by its `max_elapsed_s` and by 1e9 gives its `aggregate_GBps`, 462.29 GB/s. The rate from the T rows of that interval (the sum of `bytes / elapsed_s / 1e9` over its 120 T rows) is 462.30 GB/s.
- [Ref9] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/tmp/agent-scratch/w120-7200-2026-11-17/make_report.py`: The report generator. Its header comment names facts.json as the input (line 5). It says that the computed values are compared with FACTS.md (line 12).
- [Ref10] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/tmp/agent-scratch/w120-7200-2026-11-17/stat_bwx.py`: The earlier statistics script for bwx csv files. `interval_rows` (line 61) reads every file `*.csv` of a folder (line 64). `read_csv` (line 38) opens each file with the Python function `open` (line 43). It does not use `_open` of facts_data.py.
- [Ref11] `/home/jhan/workspace/titan/cache-perf/crb/results/B7-20261113T232859Z/B7.csv`: Example of a csv file of the first runner bwx. Each process starts with one line `-- step`. This file holds 48 such lines, and 8 of them hold a tag that starts with `common-6000-`. The file `B7.csv` of the folder `B7-20261114T024352Z` gives the same two counts. Each of the two files `C3.csv` (folders `C3-20261114T022708Z` and `C3-20261114T054116Z`) holds 18 such lines, and 4 of them hold a tag with that prefix.
